# Done

Finished backlog items, recent first, one table per lane so that lanes
finishing at the same time don't edit the same lines. Open work is in
[BACKLOG.md](../../BACKLOG.md); how an item moves here is in
[WORKFLOW.md](WORKFLOW.md) ("Doing an item"). Before 2026-10-30 these
tables were the Done section of BACKLOG.md; older history is in git and
in docs/history/ROADMAP.md's Done table.

## Closed without a PR

Rows closed when the backlog was tidied (2026-10-30): finished, moved or
superseded.

| id | item | why |
|---|---|---|
| I82 | Changing one field of a record means writing all of them (Bounce's `Body`) | Already there: `set(field, record, value)` changes one field of any record, and several fields are sets chained, `set(b, set(a, r, 1), 2)` (checked 2026-10-31 on an 8-field record). The demo builder didn't use it. Closed by the Language lane with the T7 note's PR (#764). |
| C25 | Audio: a sound is a function on blocks of samples, played live by a page's `listen` (AUDIO.md) | all five stages done (A1 to A5 below); the pedal plays at apps/pedal.html, 39× real time compiled |
| E77 | A fraction prints as `ratio(1, 2)` | done as L74 |
| L19 | Numbers stage 1 (Int as data, Rational) and stage 2 (overloading by name) | superseded: Rational was L39, one `+` for every number type is L38 and L40h's interfaces |
| L11 | Mode analysis through cases and folds (MODES.md) | L11a to L11e done; what was left, the mode shown on the `solve` node, is now E160 |
| P4 | Bigger-program rounds record language gaps | already in WORKFLOW.md ("Language gaps, every round") |
| L95 | Several named results (RESULTS.md) | language side done (L95a, L95b, L95d); the editor half is I65, the compiled half C55 |

Without `--allow` a run reaches everything, as before. Test: world-allow.test.ts; ledger with `--allow network` stops at its file, with `--allow files` runs | #1297 |
| I161 | **Bug** (found measuring E200b). Pressing on a dot in Constructions' main froze the page for about 40 s, a plain click to select it included: a wire drag asked, on the press, whether each of the definition's inputs could take the wire, and each answer is a whole check of the program (canvas.ts `connectDrag`). Now nothing is asked until the pointer moves; then the input under the pointer is asked at once and the others a few ms a frame, or not ahead at all when each answer takes long, so the drag keeps up. Selecting that dot: 40 s → 0.19 s. Test: editing.test (its wire drags); clicks on 12 nodes of Constructions and Tumble, none over 0.2 s | #1296 |

| I158 | Opening a definition froze 1 to 2 s (QA 11-03 H2). Three causes. Each of its traces (its values, main's run for a seed, a backward search) resolved the program's arithmetic again, a second each in calc: run.ts keeps the resolved program per version and check (`resolvedFor`), and relate.ts uses the same. The backward search checked the whole program again before its time limit applied, and so did a search a running program starts: the editor's check (and the runner's) is now handed in (relate.ts Options `info`). And main's long run for a seed ran before the definition drew: a run that isn't known to be quick now runs just after (main.ts `firstCall`). Open to drawn: Numbers & physics `polar` 1.1 → 0.3 s, calc `blank` 1.3 → 0.55 s, `read numbers` 1.8 → 1.0 s, Spacetime `reversed` and Tumble `flatten` 0.2 s (main's run then follows, 1 to 1.6 s). Test: editing.test, back-search, backwards, relate-wide | #1280 |

Every node's type, shown in hover titles in the blocks and the graph, is now worked out once per version of the program and of the names: `typeOf` keeps them, and the names with dot handles are the same object while nothing they depend on changes. Clicks in Constructions went from 224/188/139 ms to 138/115/95; Bounce from 135/68/46 to 131/57/28; Numbers from 86/57/62 to 64/41/30 (the three clicks of e200's script, Chromium here). The graph still redraws whole on a selection, which is the rest of E200b | #1119 |
| E204 | The editor's page was 25 MB (4 MB gzipped), all of it one script parsed before the first drawing. 19 MB of that was three runtimes written in more than once: esbuild's define puts the text at every use, and the Run worker (3 MB) had three uses, the page and audio runtimes (2.6 MB each) two each. Now each runtime is a text element of the page (`<script type="text/plain">`), and each example a JSON element, read the first time it's needed, so the browser keeps them as text and the script is 3.5 MB. The other programs' definitions in the navigator, which read every example on every redraw, are made at the first search. The page is 14.6 MB (2.6 MB gzipped), and its first definitions show after 1.5 to 2.2 s, down from 2.8 to 3.0 s (Chromium here). It is still one file, so it works opened from disk, where fetching examples when picked (the row's first idea) wouldn't. Tests bundle the examples inline as before (examplesPlugin "inline"). Checked in Chromium: the runtimes read back at their exact lengths; a search finds Relations' polar; Effects runs in the worker; Dice's page runs; Spacetime opens | #1117 |
| E205 | The example programs were wired by hand: about 80 JSON imports in main.ts, a 21-way conditional in `initialFor`, `EXAMPLES`, `START`, and editor/build.ts's own list of pages. One manifest now, editor/examples.json: each program's label, folders (merged in order, as Numbers & physics merges quadratic and physics), start definition, and whether it is also built as a page, in the picker's order. main.ts reads it for the picker and the start definitions, and makes every program the same way from its folders' files. Those come from a module editor/examplesPlugin.ts makes when the editor is bundled (build.ts and the two tests that bundle it). build.ts builds the pages it marks. Adding an example is a folder and a line | #1115 |
| E210b | Directions of a relation in the editor (POLAR.md, FEATURE-GESTURES.md): a function with results now lists its other directions on its panel, with ticks for the ports a new one gives and *Add a direction*. That makes a definition of the same ports, those as its results, marked `direction` of the relation, with one equation per result to fill (`x, y = □, □`), and opens it. A direction's own panel says which relation it belongs to and has *Make it ordinary*. New `Top.addDirection` and `Top.dropDirection` in toplevel.ts (additive). infer.ts (one line): a definition with results that still has a hole is judged when it's filled, as a solve of one already was, instead of saying "nothing gives x" for a hole. Test in toplevel.test; checked in Chromium on Relations' polar | #1113 |
| E207b | Broken main: draws.test's "Countdown numbers: every definition draws" failed since E207 (#1099). A definition with saved values that ran past the graph's new 3 s limit showed the stop as a failure in red. The graph's own time limit is not the program's fault; it now shows as a hint, as a run on values nobody gave already did ("No values on the wires: it runs longer than the 3 s the graph waits; Run main runs it in full") | #1111 |
| E210 | Ways of an interface's operation in the editor (WAYS.md, FEATURE-GESTURES.md): the operation's panel lists its ways with their costs and has *Add a way*, which makes a definition of the operation's inputs with a hole for its body, marked `way` of that field, and opens it; a way's own panel says which operation it is a way of, takes its cost, and has *Make it ordinary*. New `Top.addWay` and `Top.setWay` in toplevel.ts (additive), test in toplevel.test; checked in Chromium on a new interface. Directions are E210b | #1109 |
| E208 | Make it a function and Make it a definition were on the graph's menus only. A part's menu in the Blocks pane has them now: the part with everything written inside it (its calls) becomes the function, called where it was; a function written in place gets Make it a definition, as from its frame in the graph. NODE-EDITOR.md's audit marks both views. Test: editing.test partToFunction (fall's drift, speed · time, becomes part(speed, time)) | #1104 |
| E199 | Language features no gesture makes: a design note, docs/design/editor/FEATURE-GESTURES.md. Private constructors: yes, a tick on a case's panel (E209). Ways with their costs and a relation's directions: yes, one gesture for both since each is a definition plus one field (E210). `unique`: no, a library author's choice made in text; the type's panel shows it when set | #1101 |
| E206 | NODE-EDITOR.md's "Same edits in both views" audit listed gestures up to 2026-10-24. It now has the ones added since, each marked by reading the menu and panel code: Wrap in…, Add a step before this, Turn around, a list's sockets, result ports, Make it a function or definition, Simplify, Implement, Answered later, type parameters. One gap: Make it a function or definition, and an inner function made a definition, are on the graph's menus only (E208) | #1100 |
| E207 | Values from saved inputs, or of a definition with no inputs, were traced on the page's thread with no limit. A loop was never the risk: the termination check keeps one that might not finish from running there at all, and that stays. One that finishes after minutes would hold the page on every version of the code; it now stops after 3 s (`GIVEN_MS`; values nobody gave still stop after 200 ms) and says "it runs longer than the 3 s the graph waits; Run main runs it in full". By reading: a slow value couldn't be typed into the palette quickly enough to time it, so this is not measured | #1099 |
| E203 | On a phone a tapped part's type was out of sight: the Selected panel is in the side pane (named Run), and there's no hover. On a touch screen with one pane at a time, a tap that selects puts what it is and its type on the status line, under the pane ("Step (call) period : Float64. Its panel is in Run & more."), and the side pane's button reads **Run & more**, since it holds Run, Selected, Program, Checks, Files and Guide. A hole still switches to its palette. Checked in Chromium as a Pixel 5 | #1097 |
| E202 | Undo kept every version, each with its caches (WeakMaps keyed by the version's Code): ~6 MB of heap per edit in Constructions, without end. The undo list now keeps the last 200 versions, and a version more than 4 steps back gets a Code of its own (the same nodes under a new key), so its caches can go; an Undo that far back works them out again (16 Undos: 9.1 → 13.7 s, the last 4 as quick as before). Headless Chromium, Constructions, heap after GC over 16 Deletes: 33 → 62 → 84 → 109 → 134 MB before, 33 → 60 → 64 → 67 → 69 MB after | #1095 |
| E201 | A Delete in a big example showed nothing for 1 to 2 s: the hole it leaves is selected, and the inspector worked out everything that fits there (a check per candidate) before the edit was drawn. Any hole's palette in the inspector is now worked out after the frame that shows it (what I136 did for a new definition's hole), saying "Working out what fits here…" meanwhile; a version already worked out lists at once, and a menu opened to change something lists at once too. Headless Chromium, Bounce, keydown to the edit on screen: 1479/978/2030 → 742/729/749 ms; the list arrives when it did. The whole check per edit (infer.ts) stays E103's | #1094 |
| E200 | A click that only selects redrew the whole app at 100 to 250 ms on the big examples. Step 1: `Top.moveStep`, run twice per click only to enable Step earlier/later, scanned every node several times through `parentOf`; it and `rowOfItem` now read a parent index kept per version of the code (`parentAt`, model.ts, additive; `parentOf` still scans, since an edit in progress changes nodes in place). The graph was laid out afresh on every click; a layout is now kept per shape of graph (its nodes' sizes and places, frames, wires, steps, folded frames). Headless Chromium, ms per click (three parts, before → after): Bounce 161/136/103 → 109/99/58, Constructions 252/186/187 → 146/154/156, Numbers 129/93/76 → 76/57/45. The rest (drawing, inspector, the browser's layout) is E200b | #1091 |
| E198 | Type parameters in the editor: a data type and an effect get *Add the type parameter* (a, b, … or a typed name) and × for each, and an operation of an effect its own (`await : Ticket a → a`); the field and gives-back pickers offer them. The type's uses of itself in its fields get the new parameter (`Tree` holding a `Tree` becomes `Tree a`); a use in another type's field is refused with where, since it would need an argument nobody chose. Removing one is refused while a field names it. Selecting one renames it (the panel calls it a Type parameter, with no units or wires). New `Top.addTypeParameter`/`removeTypeParameter` in toplevel.ts (additive), test in toplevel.test; TUTORIAL.md §3.6 | #1086 |
| E197 | Saving stopped silently from about the third program: every saved doc carried its own copy of the library (1.46 M characters), so the store was full at ~4.4 M. A doc keeps only its own nodes now (the library is put back under them when it's read, as before), and a store that is still full says so on the status line and points at Save file. Three new programs: 4.44 M (the third never saved) → 52 K, all three back after a reload (test: editing.test keptOnReload). GUIDE.md §10 says what happens when the store is full | #1084 |
| E196 | Adding a case to a data type took 1.1 to 1.4 s even in a new program; half of it was drawing the type's panel, which worked out *Implement* for each interface (a whole check each) and *×* for each case before anything was clicked. Both are now worked out when clicked (a refusal says why then). Headless Chromium: creating the type 865 → 349 ms, each case 1.0 to 1.1 s → 0.52 to 0.56 s; what's left is the edit's own check. + New program (1.5 s) not looked at | #1078 |
| E194 | Without a GPU, the ray marcher's CPU frames (about 150 ms each since its motor, #1037) were drawn back to back on the page's thread, so opening a definition took 2 to 4 s. A frame is now drawn a few rows at a time, at most ~10 ms at once, and a slow one leaves the page as much time again before the next (editor/picture.ts). Measured in headless Chromium: opening `sphere` or `colour` 2.0 to 2.8 s → 0.1 to 0.7 s, and no long tasks over 3 s (21 before, 140 to 163 ms each). Opening the program itself is still ~7.6 s, the lowering's | #1076 |
| E195 | **Bug.** Typed `[1.0, 2.0]` was a `List a where a is a Number` and printed `[1, 2]`: the list was read whole as a value, which forgets the points. A value with a whole number written with a point (or an exponent) inside is now read part by part, so each is a Float64 literal (E192) and prints `1.0`; `some(2.0)` is an `Optional Float64` the same way. Test: reader.test.ts | #1073 |
| E193 | **Bug.** Undo of *+ New definition* opened the library's `Bool` (the first of the code's definitions), with nothing marked, and Redo stayed there. Each version of the program now remembers the definition open with it, the one open before the edit's own step changed it, so Undo goes back to `insert` and Redo to the new `k`; a definition Undo removed falls back to `main`. Test: editing.test.ts `undoNewDefinition` | #1071 |
| E166b | The text side of E166: a list written in place as a definition's one input reads as that call's inputs, `sum(1, 2, 3)` for `sum([1, 2, 3])`, and `p(named(…), words(" "))` for `p([…])` on several lines. The open question (it reads like a call of three inputs) is settled by the function's type, not a mark: only a definition whose one input is a List, with two or more items written in place, and whose name means nothing else, so the reader can read it back. It reads `sum(1, 2, 3)` as the list when nothing of that name takes three inputs and every meaning takes one (render.ts `spread`, typed.ts). One item keeps its brackets, `h1([words(…)])`, and so do names with other meanings (`length([1, 2])`). Prior art: Lisp's and JavaScript's rest parameters (`&rest`, `...xs`) read the same way. Test: blocks.test.ts | #1065 |
| C90 (editor half) | Opening a picture lowered it for the GPU try, made its Float32 copy and lowered that for the CPU side every time, and again when the program was opened again. Both are kept per version of the code and of what its names say (editor/picture.ts `once`), so reopening lowers nothing: editing.test's shaderOpens 21 → 15 s. The C90 row stays for staging's own check | #1061 |
| E186b | Closed with a test, no change: a stub's callers already choose a shared name's meaning. With a caller passing a Text, `length(w)` is Text's; with one passing a list, List's (a reading that breaks a caller is refused, so the next is tried). Only with no caller yet does it fall to the first meaning, List's, and nothing there says which. Test: reader.test.ts | #1060 |
| E192 | `1500.0` typed was any number (`a where a is a Number`): a whole number written with a point is now a Float64 literal, and it prints with its point (`1500.0`) so it reads back the same (typed.ts, render.ts). The other half, case analysis offering only the old type after *Clear it* and *Let it loosen*, isn't the editor's doing: the definition is in a recursive group with its callers, and inside one group a caller fixes an input's type (no polymorphic recursion), so the palette offers what checks. Reproduced in Node: g(q) and h calling g(card(…)) give q : Card. Test: reader.test.ts | #1057 |
| E191 | `≠` and `!=` weren't read ("there's no field named id ≠ id"). They now read as `not(a = b)`, since the language has no ≠ of its own (L161). A field read followed by text nothing reads now says what's left over ("after .head, “<> b” isn't read") instead of taking the rest of the line as the field's name. Test: reader.test.ts | #1055 |
| E190 | Typed text had no sequence of actions: `write(a, x); write(b, y)` was refused. A step with no name is now read as one whose value is named `_` and read by nothing, the way E183 prints it. A function whose whole body is one action, `(e) => write(a, x)`, now offers *Add a step before this…* (typed.ts `addActionStep`, `performs`). Test: reader.test.ts | #1053 |
| E188 | Typed names that read as something else. A program's own case `done` lost to std's `done(value)`: cases of one name are now told apart by how many values they take (values.ts). `random()` read as the effect's operation taken as a value, so `random() · n` was refused: an effect's operation is a call, never a value. Typing `unit` put `unit(□)` (vectors' normalize) first and Enter took it: a value named exactly what's typed now comes before a call of a function of that name, while a function still comes after its call (`random()` before `random`). Test: reader.test.ts | #1052 |
| E189 | A fresh match's cases after the first fill read as one `otherwise → □`, so a case had no hole of its own and its fields were refused. With no stored otherwise, cases whose body is still a hole never fold together (patterns.ts `sharedOtherwise`): each keeps its own hole in the text, as the graph draws it. Clearing a shared value now leaves each case its own hole too (editing.test's sharedRow says so). Test: own-case.test.ts | #1049 |
| E186 | A field read on an input nothing had settled took the first type with that field, silently (`q.title` became Card's where Quick was meant, and the refusal came two definitions later). When more than one type's reading fits, the palette now offers each, "read as a Card" / "read as a Quick", under "More than one type has these fields, and nothing here says which: pick one" (typed.ts `fieldOwners`, fillValue's `choices`; reader test). The other half, a name in a stub taking List's `length` though its one caller passes a Text, is E186b | #1047 |
| E187 | A value definition named in typed text inside a list or a constructor (`[styles, …]`, `some(save name)`, `state(starting board, …)`) was refused with infer's "points at nothing". The text read as a value, with the definition in it as a value standing for a definition (how the Run panel passes one, #703), which a node can't hold. A value that names a definition is now read as code: a reference (typed.ts; reader test) | #1044 |
| E185 | Typed `>`, `≥`, `≤` (and `>=`, `<=`) read as the prelude's hidden Float64-only `more than`, `at least`, `at most`, so `length(w) > 1` was refused. The notation (styles/blocks.json) named only the hidden copies: the generic ones now have the glyphs too, and a hidden definition is never read from typed text (typed.ts). The generic ones also print as `>`, `≥`, `≤` now | #1043 |
| I145 | The palette offered two `range`s, at Float64 and at Int, with the Float64 one first. Positions are Ints now, so of two meanings of one name in a group the Int one comes first (`range(□: Int)`, then `range(□: Float64)`). Asked by the Language lane | #1035 |
| I29 | Definitions that open with an empty Run. Last step, I29g, after a recount of converter, dice, calculus, Mandelbrot and pedal (21 of 60 still empty): a picture's pixel can miss what its helpers do (Mandelbrot's is in the set, so its palette never ran), so a helper something calls that no pixel reached is looked for at two more; a page's sound player (`listen`) plays a block of 64 samples of a sine, which reaches the pedal's helpers; a definition whose name has other marks (`as °C`, `as km/h`) reads back from a port's box (values.ts), so converter's `field` opens filled. Now 9 of 60 empty, each one nothing calls (calculus's derivatives and `x² − 2`, the pedal's `peak`): no call to borrow values from. Steps before: #578, #703, #761, #845, #900, I29e #1006, I29f #1009 | #1032 |
| I144 | Std's Pair became Tuple2 (L139), and `lab` and Open read old ids as today's, but the editor's own restore from the browser (`onToday`) didn't: a changed program that used Pair named ids that were gone, failed the check that everything resolves, and was silently replaced by the shipped example, edits lost. `onToday` now runs `onTodaysIds` on the code and names first (checked: an edited calculus saved with `std.pair…` ids keeps its edit) | #1023 |
| I137 | Make it a function on `fall`'s `+` put the new `part =` dot past the result, its wire looping back into the call. It now goes with the kept node nearest it in the fresh layout: the dot lands in the column of lets beside `drift =` and `drop =` | #1022 |
| I138 | A dot holding a function (`part : (Float64, Float64) -> Float64`, made by Make it a function) offered "Unit · part of its type" with m, s, kg… An input or dot now takes a unit only when it is a number or nothing says what it is yet | #1021 |
| I142 | Typed `match s` was refused as "nothing is named match s here". | I141 | The palette led with rows for writing representations: a new definition's calls started with `extended less`, `extended add`, …, and typing `match` listed `match by` and `match as written` above case analysis. | I140 | `first([1], else 2)` typed was refused with "first works only on a Number (Int, Rational, …)", which reads as though another meaning of `first` were meant. | I143 | *Make a definition named X* (E180) named inputs it couldn't name from the call `input 1`, `input 2`, which read as `‹input 1›` everywhere. They're `in`, or `in1`, `in2` when there are several, as *Make a function* names them | #1016 |
| I136 | + New definition took 1.5 s (physics) to 2.8 s (Constructions) from Enter to drawn. Physics 2.1 → 0.7 s, Constructions 1.8 → 1.0 s, measured from the click on Create | #1015 |
| I135 | Each key typed in the palette cost 60 to 180 ms in big programs (E173b's per-key redraw): every filling was labelled again (a type shown for each), the dot handles walked every node for what's read, and *↑ what it's in* (E183b) walked them once per step up and read every node the views draw. Labels are kept per filling while the names stay, what's read and each node's parent are found once per version of the code, and only the few candidates are looked up in the views. Physics 140 → about 28 ms a key, Spacetime 200 → about 30 (the first key of a palette still labels everything, 70 to 85 ms). Also: E183b's button sat inside the Selected panel's head, so the head read "Hole↑ what it's in" and two browser tests (heldSeed, listItems) failed on main; it's a row of its own under the head now | #1012 |

| E133 | Design ([SHARED-NODES.md](docs/design/editor/SHARED-NODES.md)): offers share the program's nodes instead of copying ~10,000 each. E133a, one scratch copy per palette (#866), and E133b, a filled program's analyzer starting from the program's slots (#875), with E103c to E103e, took physics `fall`'s worst hole from 22 s to 4 s and its 23 holes from 121 s to 25 s. Closed 2026-11-02 after a last profile ("Where it stands"): what's left is the checks' own work, 642 distinct whole checks of ~2 ms on the worst hole; cutting it needs an incremental check in infer.ts, not worth it at 4 s worst and under 1 s on most holes | #1005 |

| E183 | Two of round 7's small ones: a step added with *Add a value before this…* and filled with an action nothing reads (`for each(…)`) read `‹•1› = for each(…)` in the text, since the display handle •1 counted as a name; now a filled step nothing reads gets no handle and reads as the step itself (a step still a hole keeps its handle, to reach it). | T-palette | palette.test.ts ran past half an hour: its three sweeps ("every filling the palette offers in calculus, backwards, queens type-checks") took holes from every call in the program, the library's ten thousand nodes included, and checked each of a hole's hundred-odd fillings with a whole check. They now take the example's own calls, as their names say: the file runs in about 2 minutes | #995 |
| E175b | E175 put a meaning that leaves an open type open first, and builtin `total` (any record of Float64s) counted as leaving it open, so tumble's typed `total(board)` in a new function took the builtin over tumble's own `total : (Board) -> Float64` and the input stayed `a` (mcp.test.ts failed on main). A variable held to numbers or a record of them now counts as settling the type, as a type does; only plain data (show for any value) leaves it open | #994 |
| E178 | Typed text couldn't write what the text prints: `first(x, else y)` (only `first(() => x, () => y)`), a list of anything but literals (`[k]`, `[show(1)]`), a step (a value used twice), a `□` left to fill later. The palette now reads them back: a word the notation prints before an input is read past, an action given by name is wrapped where every meaning takes a function of nothing, `[a, b, ..rest]` of any expressions, `name = value; rest` as a step, and `□` as a hole. `match` is still not read | #991 |
| E176 | A typed line of `++` with shows and field reads (sheet's `"line " ++ show(e.line) ++ ": " ++ e.name ++ " is " ++ cell text(results, e.name)`) was refused though each part went in alone: of its 128 combinations of meanings (`line` a field of two types, `name` of four, `show` four meanings) only the first 16 were tried. Each name's meanings are now narrowed by the types around them before combining: a meaning whose input wants a type the part there can't be is dropped, and a value nothing has typed yet (`e`) can be only what every place it's given to takes | #988 |
| E177 | A fresh `match x · T` read as `otherwise → □` in the text: one hole standing for every case, the first fill made in all of them, the cases' binders hidden until *Give … its own body*, while the graph drew a hole per case (sheet split 96 cases by hand). A match whose cases are all still holes now reads a row and a hole per case, binders shown, in the text as in the graph; holes left by clearing a value several cases shared still read as one otherwise | #987 |
| E173b | E173's pause kept the palette's list as it was while typing: the keys showed, the list didn't follow them until the pause. Now each key redraws the list at once from names alone (the typed reading held, the hint saying "Reading what's typed…"), and the reading follows once typing pauses; Enter before the pause reads first, then takes the top choice. 41 keys: 3.3 s, the value shown 0.9 s later | #986 |
| E175 | A name with several meanings, typed, took the first that type-checked, which could settle a type nothing had settled yet: `show(n)` of an `n` nothing else used was Float64's, so `n` was a Float64 and a later `entry(…, n)` at Int was refused. Meanings that would fix the type of a value still open (a parameter nothing uses yet, a stub's result) are now tried after those that leave it open: `show(n)` is show for any value, and `n` stays open. Where every meaning settles it (List's or Text's `length` of a stub) the first is still taken | #983 |
| E174 | A refused typed expression always said "Nothing that fits matches. Values and arithmetic can be typed too…", whatever the reason. The palette now shows the reading's own reason, first, when the text is an expression or nothing listed matches it: "speed ++ time doesn't fit here: fall is pinned to (Float64, …) -> Bool, and this makes it (Text, Text, Float64, Text) -> Bool". A sum whose last part calls a name that isn't there now names that part ("there's no function named nosuch"), not the whole text | #981 |
| E173 | Typing in the palette read the typed text again (`fillValue`, a whole check per reading) on every key and every redraw, and the text stayed when another definition opened, read there against its first hole: 81 keys took 114 s in round 7. Now typing redraws once it pauses (250 ms), each program, hole and text is read once (cached by the checked program and names), and opening a definition empties the box. physics `fall`, 41 keys typed 40 ms apart: 9.8 s of typing became 1.8 s, the reading showing 0.8 s after the last key | #979 |
| I126b | I126's fix moved only a frame made wholly of new nodes. With two values picked (physics `fall`'s drift and drop, as the QA did it), the new call, its reads and the tuple's dot still sat on the nodes that stayed (5 overlaps). Now each new unit, either a frame of new nodes taken as one block or a new node of its own, that still covers something moves up or down to the nearest free place, or else below everything. What stays doesn't move. `fall` with both values: 0 overlaps; with one: 0 | #973 |
| I129 | "Work out … from the result" (E168) was on every call's menu but refused on almost every example: Relations' `split` and `to polar` and physics `main`'s `fall` give a Pair or a Bool, which none of their inputs is, and the reason read "a given port: Bool isn't a Number". The menu now leaves out an input whose type isn't the result's (checked from the types, so the menu stays quick), and a turn the search can't make says so: "missing addend can't work out known from its result: nothing in its body gives known back when the rest is given" | #972 |

| I127 | Ctrl+Z right after adding a list item from its + socket did nothing: the new hole's palette search box had the focus, and the editor left Ctrl+Z in a field to the browser. Page: two dice → `main`: the item added, Ctrl+Z, the code is back | #963 |
| I126 | Make it a function drew the new function's frame over the nodes that stayed (physics `fall`: over `drift =`), because new nodes were spaced out one at a time, each only a short way. The 2 s the edit takes is the whole-program checks every edit runs (E103, stopped at the 10% rule), not the placing | #962 |
| I128 | A node's context menu took 0.45 to 0.75 s to show and 1 to 2 s more to grey its items out: every check (Make it a function, Make it a definition, Add a value before, the dot…) tried its edit on the whole program in turn. Each item is now checked when it is pointed at or reached by the keyboard; pressed unchecked, the edit says why it can't. physics `fall`'s `+`: the menu shows after about 0.1 s, with about 0.1 s of work behind it (was about 4 s) | #960 |
| I125 | Nine of Bounce's helpers opened on a failed search, their wires saying "a Float64 that isn't finite reached arithmetic". Not the finite Float64: main runs them as copies at other number types (arith.ts; `+` on multivectors), while the definition as written defaults to Float64's, so the wire values added multivectors as Float64s. When main's run entered a copy and the definition as written fails on those values, the wire values (trace.ts) now try its copies and show the one that runs on the nodes it copies (97 of `moved`'s 100 nodes). The runner gives its copies and tells `entered` which copy it entered (run.ts, additive). Test: trace-copies.test.ts | this PR |
| I124 | Pig's `hold at` and `quietly` opened on a failed search, their wires saying "nothing handles np2oerr2…". Such a definition is no longer run or searched on its own; its panel says "It handles Decide for the function it's given…: Run main to see it at work". The runner's "nothing handles" names the operation | this PR |
| I123 | Right-clicking a value given to a solve (`y` in Relations' `square roots`) threw `… has no parent` from Make it a function's menu check: a solve's given values weren't children to `parentOf` and `swapIn` (model.ts), which now know them (slot `given`, by port). A menu check that throws now greys its own item with the reason, and the checks after it still run. Test: make-function.test.ts | this PR |
| I122 | Values on the wires of ray marching's six helpers (`shapes`, `march`, `shadow`, `sky`, `shade`, `trace`) said "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'fields')": the wire-values pass (trace.ts) walked the program as written, calling − on two vectors, while the runner had resolved it to their parts (arith.ts). A call the runner changed is now run as the runner runs it (the runner gives its code, run.ts), and what went into it is still shown. Test: toplevel.test.ts | this PR |

## C: Compiler and targets

| id | item | PR |
|---|---|---|
| C148 | `show any` through a type's Shown implementation in compiled code (L205, SPEC: the evaluator is the reference): compiled JavaScript's `$showAny` is given each case of a type with one and its implementation (`$shown`), so a value of that type, alone or inside a list or record, shows as the evaluator shows it; the core IR's show (WebAssembly, C, JavaScript from the core IR) calls the implementation's `show` for a type of the program's own (std's number types still show as before there). The implementations are reached when a show is (scalar.ts `reachedWithShows`, std.ts `shownImplementations`, shared with the evaluator). own-arithmetic.test.ts: a version shows as 1.9 in all three | this PR |
| C147 | A use's given ports in port order in compiled code (SPEC §7.2b, L198): compile.ts's JavaScript computed them in the order the file keeps them (an `out` written first printed first); now compile.ts (`givenOf`, the relational interpreter's call, `compileSolve`'s known ports), modes.ts's written-out modes, scalar.ts and core-js.ts all take ports.ts `givenInOrder`, as the evaluator does. compile.test.ts: a use whose `out` and `b` print, written out of order, prints b, out, 3 in the evaluator, compiled JavaScript and JavaScript from the core IR | #1333 |
| C137c17 | The core IR's lowering for JavaScript (core-js.ts `lowerCore`, used by `lab compile --core`, `lab build-web --core` and the tests) takes the program after handlers.ts's pass, as compile.ts does (`fusedProgram`, now shared): a handler whose cases answer in place is written into the code it handles, so effects (its own Ask) runs and agrees with the evaluator, 21 examples. pig's and tumble's handlers aren't of that kind yet | #1332 |
| C137c16 | `for some(x => …)` in JavaScript from the core IR: the evaluator's relational interpreter (relate.ts `satisfiable`) on the printed closure, which carries its lambda (C137c13), with the program's nodes in the module. core-js.test.ts checks it against the evaluator on equations with and without answers (x·x = c, 2t + 1 = a with t < b). physics still stops at polar's reading (C146) | #1330 |
| C137c15 | JavaScript from the core IR says no answer with a value, not a thrown exception: a partial function gives `R.FAIL`, checked where a statement reads it (a let's value, an if's test, a match, a call's first inputs), which leaves the function or takes the nearest attempt's otherwise; an attempt in a function's last step passes its own FAIL on. Inside an expression, a budget or a handler, and to the evaluator's runtime (a closure called from outside, a function given out), it is still thrown. countdown 3.1 s → 0.6 s (compile.ts's: 0.2 s); queens, sort, routes and generic about 0.2 to 0.35 s, which is mostly loading the bundled runtime | #1329 |
| C137c14 | JavaScript from the core IR makes data written out whole (an operator list, a case with no fields) once, at load, instead of at every call. Measured against compile.ts's JavaScript, run with Node: countdown 204 ms against 5.2 s before and 3.5 s after; queens 81 against 372 and 332; sort 59 against 227 and 260, which is mostly loading the bundled evaluator runtime (4.6 MB). Next: a failure as a value, not a thrown exception (`R.fail` is a third of countdown's time) | #1325 |
| C145b | The fuzz test generates a search under a budget (`first(() => with fuel(n, search), () => …)`, n of 0, 3 or 200): several answers, the first taken, or none, which gives the otherwise; about one in every thirty programs, so C145 would have been found. Seeds 1 to 5 agree | #1320 |
| C146 | Bug guard: a relation whose type is one reading of it (L130: physics' polar, typed from x and y to its length and angle) was lowered with its type's inputs while its body read all four ports; it was reached only once `hence` lowered. `func` now refuses one whose lambda has more ports than its type has inputs, saying so. `hence` lowers as `and`, as it runs (std.ts), on every core IR target; physics' main now stops at polar's reading | #1319 |
| C144 | Library maths from the platform in C (SPEC §3.2, S12, Ersagun 2026-11-03): a native program calls libm's sin, cos, tan, exp, log, pow and atan2 instead of carrying V8's fdlibm; `emitC`'s `math: "fdlibm"` still carries it, the same bits as Node's Math, and the agreement tests (fuzz, c.test.ts's harness) build that way. c.test.ts checks both on 140,000 calls: fdlibm to the bit, glibc's libm within 4 ulp (it differs in the last bits on about 3,800). WebAssembly keeps the host's Math in a browser and fdlibm under WASI, which has none | #1318 |
| C145 | Bug: `first(() => with fuel(n, () => body), () => otherwise)` whose body has no answer gave no answer in WebAssembly, C and JavaScript from the core IR, where the evaluator gives `otherwise` (first sees no answer either way). With `data`, the body is now an `optional` search under the budget, matched after it, so no answer and running out both give `otherwise`, which runs outside the budget as in the evaluator; a body with several answers under such a budget lowers too (it was refused). Found by C137's printer; fixtures.ts `several` gains both, WebAssembly agrees on 106 cases | #1317 |
| C137c13 | JavaScript from the core IR: a printed closure carries where it came from (`closure.src` in the core IR: the lambda and the binders its captures are, a definition's lambda, or the builtin), as the evaluator's do (run.ts META), so the evaluator's solver looks inside a function it is given (inverse). converter and spacetime run and agree with the evaluator, pages too (16 examples, 6 pages) | #1309 |
| C137c12 | JavaScript from the core IR: the relational interpreter's several answers are `one of` a list made as it is read, its search spending the printed program's budget (`R.spending`) as the evaluator's spends its fuel; a builtin used as a value is a closure calling the evaluator's (`builtinValue`); a solve given a function is refused by name, since the solver looks inside it (inverse, in conjugate, converter, pedal and spacetime). The fuzz test lowers for this printer with `host`, as `lab compile --core` does: seeds 1 to 40 agree | this PR |
| C141 | Shaders and library maths in the spec (S6, S12), decided by Ersagun 2026-11-03: the shader profile is a declared approximation with its own meaning (SPEC §10.3), and library functions (sin, exp, pow…) are the platform's own within a few ulp, not carried by every program (§3.2); a program needing the same bits everywhere asks by its type. Rows C144 (C and the tests) and L210 (a portable Float64 type) | #1298 |
| C137c11 | Solves in JavaScript from the core IR: a relation run some way is the evaluator's solve, `solveAnswers` (moved out of run.ts's machine so both use it), on the program's nodes, which `lab compile --core` and `lab build-web --core` carry only when there is a solve. Where the definition has no Choice and no search is around, its first answer is the one; otherwise the solver's answers are `one of` a list made as it is read (to polar's angle has no end). The relational interpreter's several answers are still refused. relations runs and agrees with the evaluator (14 examples), as a module too | #1295 |
| C137c10 | JavaScript from the core IR answers `now` and a draw nothing seeded from the world, as the evaluator does (rt.world's `now` and `random`), so dice lowers: its page shows the same HTML as the evaluator's before and after its handlers run, given the same clock and draws | #1287 |

| I106c | A rest of a two-ended array was always a view on the whole buffer, so a list taken apart item by item kept its first buffer (a million slots for one item left; Go's slices leak the same way). Each copy holds three quarters of the one before at most, so the copies add up to under three times the list: still constant per rest. WebAssembly and C free a buffer by counts and keep it while any view holds it, as before. A fetch that failed comes back to the await as `$Failed`, and the await performs the Abort itself, so `attempt` around it catches it, as in the evaluator. A page's world can't wait, so a page still fetches when the fetch starts. | C94 | GAPS round 9, M4, the rest: in C, a record or data input that a function only reads fields of, or lends on in turn, is lent, not given. The caller passes it without a dup, and the function never drops it (Perceus's borrowed parameters, Reinking, Xie, de Moura and Leijen, PLDI 2021). Such a function is written as `name_b`, and calls in the program use it. `name`, the one the host and closures call, lends its inputs to it and drops them after. A function that makes a new value of the input's type is an update (machine's `stored`) and keeps its input, so the fields it moves on have one holder and are changed in place. A function that loops keeps its inputs too. Machine, `lab native`, median of 3: fib 20 146 → 103 ms, collatz 3000 562 → 480, sort 400 195 → 179, primes 5000 45 → 42. Test: c.test.ts C94 (`value of` is called as `lab_value_of_b(p0, p1)`, no `Machine_dup`), and every C test's check that each cell is freed | #1186 |
| C115 | The generated programs (C101) reach maps, sets and queues: `as map` of `zipped` lists, `put`, `value at`, `remove key`, `has key`, `merge maps`, `keys of`, `values of`, `largest key`, `map size`; `as set`, `set union`, `set difference`, `has item`, `items of`, `smallest item`; `queue of`, `queued`, `dequeued`, `queue size`, `queue items`. Each definition is also called with a fourth set of inputs: an Int past 64 bits (2^64 + 1), 1e300, and a list holding 2^62 and −2^63. 70 seeds × 60 definitions and 12 seeds × 150 are clean on every engine; nothing found this time. Test: fuzz.test.ts | #1185 |
| C114 | Bug found by the generated programs (C101): `sum(take(nil, n))` would not load in WebAssembly (a tail call of the wrong type). Arithmetic resolution (arith.ts) makes a copy of a generic definition for each number type it is used at, but not for a use at the definition's own default. It took that default from the scheme's variable, while a library definition's body is typed with variables of its own, and only those carry the mark a whole literal leaves. So `sum`'s body was at Int (its `0`), and a use at Float64 was sent to it uncopied. The default is now read through the body's own variables. The generator also gains `set size` of `as set`, `last of items`, `inserted at`, `range from`, `flat map`, `repeated`, and calls of earlier definitions; 80 seeds × 60 definitions are clean. Tests: wasm.test.ts and c.test.ts "what the generated programs found" (fixtures.ts `fuzzFinds` gains `summed nothing`) | #1181 |
| C113 | Bug found by the generated programs (C101): `length(sort(nil))` was refused by WebAssembly and C. In data mode a type variable left free became Float64 only at the top of a type, so the `List a` inside stayed free. Now a free variable becomes Float64 wherever it sits in a type's arguments or a function's types. The generator also gains `sin`, `exp`, `atan2`, `power`, `sum`, `whole part`, `sort`, `uppercase`, `starts with`, `contains`, and `join all` of a `split`; 60 seeds × 60 definitions are clean. Test: c.test.ts "what the generated programs found, in C" (fixtures.ts `fuzzFinds`) | #1175 |
| C112 | Bug found by the generated programs (C101): `join all(split(t, ","), "+")` failed to build in C, because the list-of-text type a runtime function takes or gives was declared only when the program itself made such a list. The C build now declares it whenever a runtime function uses it. Test: the same c.test.ts test, with every cell freed | #1175 |
| C109 | Bug found by the generated programs (C101): `seeded(1e300, …)` drew other numbers in WebAssembly than in the evaluator. The seed is JavaScript's ToUint32 of its floor (random.ts), but WebAssembly saturated it to an i64 first, and C relied on an out-of-range conversion that only happened to agree. Both now take y − ⌊y / 2^32⌋·2^32, which is exact for every Float64. The generator also gains `read number`, `read` (Int), `code`, `find`, `show` of a Float64 and of an Int, `from code`, a seeded pair of draws, `keep` with a closure, and number-like text leaves; 70 seeds × 60 definitions are clean. Test: fixtures.ts `randomInputs` gains seeds 1e300, −1e300, 2^63, −2^33 + 5 and 2^70 + 2^40 (WebAssembly differed before) | #1168 |
| C108 | `read number` (L176) in C and WebAssembly. Both check text.ts's grammar (a sign, digits with perhaps a point, perhaps an exponent). C then calls strtod, which rounds once. WebAssembly reads the digits as an exact magnitude d and a power of ten e: for e ≥ 0 it rounds d·10^e once (mag.toNumber, ties to even); for e < 0 it takes d·2^s over 10^-e with at least 65 bits and the remainder as a last bit, then scales; at or below the smallest normal it takes the multiple of 2^-1074 nearest instead, so subnormals are rounded once too. Past the largest Float64 there is no answer, and -0 reads as 0. Also fixed: a module whose only big-number code is a runtime function on magnitudes didn't declare their array type. Tests: fixtures.ts `numberTexts` (the grammar's edges, halfway cases, the largest, subnormals, 1e±100000) in the WebAssembly "one answer or none" test and in C with every cell freed; 120,000 random decimals checked against the evaluator in WebAssembly by hand. Prior art: Clinger, "How to read floating point numbers accurately" (PLDI 1990), the exact big-number reading | #1165 |
| C40 step 2g | A function that waits and chooses, given to the library, in compiled JavaScript: `map(xs, x ↦ { yield(x); one of([x, 0]) })` inside a search crashed the compiler ("has Choice but is compiled as a plain expression") and `keep` with such a test was refused. The lambda is now a generator of its answers and its waits, as a search that waits is (step 2a); a call of a function given (not a definition) inside a generator passes its waits on; and a copy whose function input waits waits too even when its own row is another variable (`keep`'s test is called inside its own `all`, so keep's row is the test's without Choice). Test: later.test.ts "given to map and to keep", the evaluator's prints, and probes of a waiting search calling a search that doesn't wait, mapping a choosing function, a pair taken apart and recursion building a list (all already right) | #1162 |
| C107 | Closed after measuring (GAPS round 10 K3): after C105, clinic at 200 hours compiled to JavaScript spends 20% in the garbage collector, but most of it is the heaps, lists and texts each event makes, not the `Clinic` record. The upper bound, every set of a ten-field record changed in place (unsound, the output differs), took about 1.59 s to 1.53 s (0 to 7%, inside the noise). Reuse in place in JavaScript needs ownership known across functions (`with desk`, `noted`, `started` each take the record and give a new one), which is a large change for less than the lane's 10% bar. C already reuses a cell with one holder (its reuse tokens, C94) | #1157 |
| C105 | `set` in compiled JavaScript without a path walk (GAPS round 10 K2): a set is a function of the program's own, one per path and record type, `(r, v) => ({ tag: r.tag, fields: [r.fields[0], v, …] })`, instead of the runtime's `setPath(r, [1], v)` with its path array, `slice()` and spread; a chain of sets on one record (`set(a, set(b, r, x), y)`, a path inside another, the same field twice) is one function, so the record is copied once, and the values are still worked out in the order written. Clinic at 200 hours: about 1.8 s to 1.66 s (5 to 18%, noisy), no `setPath` left in it. Sets chained through lets (`c13 = set(…c12…)`, `c23 = set(…c13…)`) are still a copy each: C107. Test: compile.test.ts "Int, text and set" gains two fields chained, one field twice, and a whole field then a field inside it, the evaluator's text and no `$setPath(` in the source | #1154 |
| C106 step 2 | `set(field, record, value)` in WebAssembly and C (GAPS round 10 K4): the path is fields written in place (the checker insists), so scalar.ts `setField` makes the record again along it with that field changed and the rest kept, the walk `changed` (C100) already does for a path of fields; several fields at once (L167) were already nested sets. With step 1, clinic builds with `lab native` and gives `lab run`'s report and CSV byte for byte (0.01 s against 2.5 s). Test: fixtures.ts `changes` gains a field, a path through a Box to its machine's pc, and two fields at once, the evaluator's values in WebAssembly and in C with every cell freed; the generated programs (C101) now set a box's fields. Prior art: the lens view of record update (Foster et al., "Combinators for bidirectional tree transformations", 2007), already in records.ts | #1150 |
| C106 step 1 | Random under `seeded` in WebAssembly and C (GAPS round 10 K4): ters/src/lang/random.ts's generator, xoshiro128** seeded by SplitMix32, in the runtimes (wasm-rt.ts `rand.*` on four i32 globals, c-rt.ts `lab_rand_*`), so every target draws the evaluator's numbers. `seeded(seed, body)` keeps the generator's words as it found them, seeds it, runs the body and puts them back, so an outer seeded goes on where it was; `random()` is the next number. A function whose row has Random is lowered (it runs under some seeded); a root that draws with nobody seeding it is refused, by name (the world's Random isn't there yet). An abort out of a seeded skips putting the words back: an outer seeded then draws from the inner one's generator (noted, not fixed). Test: fixtures.ts `randoms` (three draws at seeds up to 2³²+1, a seeded inside another, a helper that draws called from map's function), the evaluator's numbers in WebAssembly and in C with every cell freed. Prior art: Blackman and Vigna's xoshiro128** (2018, public domain); Steele, Lea and Flood's SplitMix (OOPSLA 2014), already credited in random.ts | #1143 |

| C101b | The generated programs (C101) make Text (concat, slice, length, show, compared), a data type of three cases (Shape: matched, compared, shown), a record (Box: its fields, compared) and folds over a list, with the answer in scope. They found three more bugs, fixed here: (1) C leaked a cell when a match on a cell nobody else holds kept it for a new cell of the same fields, a way out of the arm freed it on the first try, and the second try (without it) still kept it (c.ts `takeApart`: what the first try did with it is undone); (2) WebAssembly trapped on an Int past an i64 given as a text position or code (`slice(t, 0, 2⁶⁴)`): such an Int is now the nearest end (wasm-rt.ts `int.clamped`), as text.ts clamps; (3) WebAssembly and C refused equal on values of a type nothing constrains (the items of a fold of `[]`): held as Float64, as C101 did for their lists. A Text result is given as is: `show` of a Text, quoted, isn't in WebAssembly and C yet (C102). 60 seeds of 60 definitions agree | #1133 |
| C101 | A generated differential test (REVIEW-2026-11-02c §3, asked for by the coordinator): small random typed definitions over Float64, Int, Bool and List Int (arithmetic, comparisons, if, let, a match on a list, map with a closure, the prelude's list jobs, `/`, `sqrt`, `log`, `mod`, `quotient` and `remainder` under `first`, numbers up to 1e300 and 2⁶²), each shown as text, run on the evaluator, compiled JavaScript, WebAssembly and C (compiler/test/fuzz.test.ts, fuzz-programs.ts). The evaluator's text is the meaning; an overflow the evaluator stops on may go unreported where nobody uses it (FLOAT-VALUES §2). 40 definitions in one program, so each engine builds once: about 3 s. `SEED=n` runs another set, `FUZZ=n` makes n. It found five bugs, fixed here: (1) compiled JS wrote an unused `let` of a record as `{ … };`, a block, a syntax error (compile.ts `bindLet`); (2) WebAssembly and C refused `[]` whose items nothing constrains, and map over it (scalar.ts: such a part is held as Float64, as no value of it is made; a root's own type variables stay refused, since its callers give values of them); (3) an Int literal past an i64 (1e300 as an Int) made WebAssembly fail to load (wasm.ts: a `$huge` of its limbs); (4) C wrote labels nothing jumps to, an error under -Werror (c.ts); (5) WebAssembly's `quotient` and `remainder` of an Int past an i64 by 0 trapped instead of having no answer (wasm-rt.ts). The C harness moved from c.test.ts to c-harness.ts to be shared. 60 seeds of 80 definitions agree. Prior art: Csmith (Yang, Chen, Eide and Regehr, PLDI 2011); QuickCheck's generators (Claessen and Hughes, 2000) | #1122 |
| C99 | `case name` (L162) in WebAssembly and C, where it was refused as data: show's walk (scalar.ts `shown`) stopped at the tag. Test: fixtures.ts `caseNames` (the program's own cases, Optional's, a list's, a Bool, a record, a tuple, a number, a text), the evaluator's names in WebAssembly (wasm.test.ts) and in C with every cell freed (c.test.ts) | #1116 |
| C100 | `changed(value, path, part)` (L163) in WebAssembly and C, where it was refused as a cell operation though it is pure. With `data`, a path written out where it's used (`path` of a chain of fields, `item path`, `then path`) is read when compiling, and the value is rebuilt along it: a field step makes the record again with that field changed, an item step a function of its own that walks the list to the item (scalar.ts `changed`). A place that isn't there leaves the value as it was, as cells.ts changeAt does; a path computed at run time is refused with why. Test: fixtures.ts `changes` (a field, an item, one inside the other, an item past the end, a field of an item, an item of an item's field, the item 199,990 of 200,000), the evaluator's text in WebAssembly (wasm.test.ts) and in C with every cell freed (c.test.ts) | #1112 |

PLDI 2021). On top of C93's trees it bought no reliable gain: best of 7 runs, three processes each, collatz 3000 282-302 → 286-374 ms, primes 20000 199-246 → 186-197 ms, sort 1000 878-948 → 773-1020 ms, inside the noise; V8 makes a short-lived 8-field array cheaply. Below the 10% rule, so not merged. The measured 30% was on hand-edited arrays, before the trees | #1102 |
| C93 step 1 | GAPS round 9, M1: machine's registers, memory and code are lists read and written by position, and the chooser gave List up program-wide for three reasons machine has. A list of lists is no longer one (a list's rows never look at its items; only a list in a map's keys or a set's items counts). With Map and Set chosen first, lists are chosen again on what that made, those rows asked for as the program's own, so a Map or Set stored another way is no library holder, and its meaning stays as written. The boundary's library copies no longer change the originals. main's list inputs are stored on the way in. `map` is a job of the tree and array rows (linear; the tree's is log n deep where a map by match went n deep). Compiled primes 20000 47.9 → 0.47 s, `lab native` sort 1000 30.4 → 2.2 s and primes 20000 36 → 0.47 s, all agreeing with the ideal (compiler/test/machine.test.ts); every example agrees three ways (examples-agree, SWEEP=all); chapters now takes arrays and checks | #1093 |
| C98 | L160 (Language lane), P1: a search whose function input shares its row, `(a) -[Choice, e]-> Bool`, passes it on to itself, so a lambda that doesn't choose went the C80 way (plain where the copy's e has no Choice) though the row names Choice itself, and the copy's `for (const r of t(x))` threw on it. compile.ts now leaves such a lambda a stream. Test: compiler/test/choice-callback.test.ts | #1087 |
| C94 step 1 | GAPS round 9, M4: in C, machine's `stepped` read `m.pc` after `executed(…, m)`, so the machine was dup'd for the call and every list in it had two holders while an instruction ran; `stored` changed `m.memory` while still holding `m`. Fields still needed are now read before the record is given to a call, and at the end of a function a record read field by field while one field is changed has every field read first (after the calls that take it whole), so the changed list has one holder. Perceus's borrowing (Reinking, Xie, de Moura and Leijen, PLDI 2021) is the prior art. primes 20000 native 61 → 36 s, every program's output the same. Test: compiler/test/c.test.ts "a record's fields are read before it is given away" | #1083 |

Tests: ters/test/library-reps.test.ts (I105) | #827 |

A pop-and-push program that also reads by position keeps cons cells. The measured cost test per row is I106b. Tests: ters/test/library-reps.test.ts (I106) | #832 |

A row whose code drifts from its table fails before the chooser trusts it. Tests: ters/test/rep-costs.test.ts | #839 |

| C63a | The one-holder check for IN-PLACE.md step 2: does every Map in a program have one holder, so a table could be changed in place unseen? A value is used once by whatever changes it; reads (`value at`, `has key`, a field) borrow and must come first; a helper's parameters get a mode (read, taken, shared) found to a fixpoint; closures may only borrow, and one that is kept may capture none; a map of maps and a definition with Choice are refused. routes, ledger, chapters and wc pass; keeping an old version, giving a map twice, or keeping it in a closure fail. Prior art: Wadler's linear types (1990), Rust's borrowing, Perceus and Lean 4's reuse. Nothing chooses with it yet (C63b). Tests: ters/test/one-holder.test.ts | #843 |
| C30d2 | std Array's two ends in WebAssembly and C: `first of array` (no answer when empty), `rest of array` and `push front` on the same two-ended buffer; a push front onto the newest array writes into the free slot before it, onto a shared one copies, and in C a view nobody else holds moves on in place. A million pushes at the front run in linear time on both. Shaders refuse them for now (a shader's array has one size where it's made) | #754 |
| L125c1 | A Float64 is finite on compiled JavaScript, as in the evaluator (FLOAT-VALUES-2026-11-01.md §2): no answer is checked on the argument (`$div`, `$sqrt`, `$log`, `$pow`, `$mod`; none for a literal that can't be one), and an overflow late, with `$fin`, where it could vanish or decide something: a comparison, `equal`, `show`, `compare`, a divisor, `exp`, `atan2`, `range`, `whole part`. The stops are Aborts performed where the operation was, with the evaluator's reasons, so a handler of Abort in the program gets them; an overflow nothing uses isn't reported (Haskell's imprecise exceptions). `atan2` takes −0 as 0, as the evaluator does. Tests: compiler/test/finite.test.ts | #850 |
| L125c2 | A Float64 is finite on WebAssembly and C too: the lowering (scalar.ts) checks each Float64 operation the program calls as compiled JavaScript does (no answer on the argument, overflow late at a comparison, a divisor, `exp`, `atan2`, `show`, `range`, `whole part`), with a `stop` op whose reasons (compiler/src/stops.ts) are the evaluator's. WebAssembly's host throws the Abort, WASI writes the reason and exits with 1 (`wasi.stop`), C's `lab_stop` does the same, and C's test harness takes a stop as that call's answer. Unlike JS, a handler in the program doesn't get it; shaders have no checks. `atan2` takes −0 as 0 there too. The 9 WASM and C tests that fed ∞ or NaN since #842 now use finite inputs and compare the stops. Tests: compiler/test/finite.test.ts (WebAssembly, WASI), c.test.ts, wasm.test.ts | #867 |
| C63b | Map as a hash table changed in place (IN-PLACE.md step 2): a new row in LIBRARY.reps, `hash table`, on std's `Table` (a JS Map; its entries come out in compare's order, so its meaning is the sorted Map). The chooser weighs it only where the program runs on a host Map (the evaluator and compiled JavaScript: `lab run`, `compile`, `build-web`, `build-app`; never `lab native`), every map has one holder (C63a's check) and every key is an Int or a Text; a representation says so with the new `in place()` job. The empty map is one shared constant, so a put on it copies. A counting program (200,000 puts and lookups) runs about 8× faster compiled. routes still gets the tree, since the chooser counts each job once and routes lists its keys (C63c). Tests: compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts | #870 |
| C63c | The representation chooser weighs a job that runs in a loop by n (represent.ts `looped`): one inside a fold's arms, inside a function given to another (a thunk given to `first` is not one), or in a definition that calls itself, and everything those reach. A lookup per town costs n log n on a tree and n on a table, and outweighs a listing of the keys done once. So routes takes Map's hash table on JavaScript, printing the same: 319 ms against 654 ms on the trees at 3000 towns (8.8× the ideal instead of 18×). bench-routes.ts has a `compiled, table` column. | L125c3 | Compiled JavaScript gives no answer where the evaluator does (L125b): a call to `/`, `sqrt`, `log`, `^` or `mod` that the checker didn't find always answered (`info.answered`) is a Choice, through `$divN`, `$sqrtN`, `$logN`, `$powN` and `$modN`, which give `$no` (builtins.ts `NO_ANSWER_JS`). So `first(() => a / b, () => -1)` is -1 at b = 0, and a handler of Abort no longer sees x / 0. An overflow still stops. Countdown 196 to 115 ms, ledger on 200,000 entries 807 to 488 ms; native is fastest everywhere, at a tenth of the memory or less. Numbers in NATIVE.md | #316 |
| C17 N4b | Native programs, stage 4b: whole programs in C. The console and files over the C library and POSIX, with Node's world's words for failures; main gets the arguments and runs on a thread with a 1 GiB stack (calc's 100,000 nested parentheses), its Int the exit code, an unhandled Abort's reason on standard error with 1. `lab native <program> [-o out]` writes the C and builds it. wc, ledger, chapters, calc, queens and the world fixture give the evaluator's output, errors, exit codes and files on 70 runs | #315 |
| C17 N4a | Native programs, stage 4a: Int, Text and Abort in C (compiler/src/c-rt.ts). An Int is one word, the value while it fits 62 bits, else a counted cell of limbs (Knuth's Algorithm D as Hacker's Delight writes it, credited); a Text a counted UTF-8 cell; Number shown as JavaScript's String; Abort as status 2 with handlers as labels. Calls are statements, in order (an argument's call may drop a cell another argument reads). Calc's big Int arithmetic, tokens and parser, chapters, wc, ledger's amounts and aborts, and the converter give the evaluator's answers with every cell freed | #314 |
| C17 N3 | Native programs, stage 3: functions as values, one answer or none, and searches in C (docs/design/targets/NATIVE.md). Closures are counted cells holding their captures, called through a typed pointer and owning the closure; a partial function sets a status and gives a stand-in value, each call checks it and leaves through a label that drops what it owns; first/optional/all/count and failing budgets are a body, then a label for none. Countdown's whole search (solutions), algebra's evaluate, simplify and newton, calculus's partial definitions and the Peano and binary numbers give the evaluator's answers with no cell left, under AddressSanitizer too. Found on the way: ±∞ counted as whole numbers in the shared lowering (so WebAssembly and shaders too), now as Number.isInteger; `and`/`or` evaluate both sides in C as in WebAssembly | #313 |
| C17 N2 | Native programs, stage 2: data in C with reference counts (docs/design/targets/NATIVE.md). Cells per case, a static one for each case with no fields; dup and drop from liveness while printing (a use is the last or a dup, a branch drops what it doesn't use, a field read borrows), Perceus's (Koka) liveness half; a cell's fields are freed from a stack, not by recursion. sort, the calculus polynomials (100,000 coefficients), Countdown's valid, algebra's derivative, equal on lists and a rose tree's folds give the evaluator's answers, with no cell left after each call, also under AddressSanitizer | #312 |
| C17 N1 | Native programs, stage 1 (design: docs/design/targets/NATIVE.md, C from the same lowering with Perceus-style counts): the scalar profile and records print as C (compiler/src/c.ts), structs by value, self tail calls as loops, budgets; calculus, physics, the ray marcher's colours and the strip integral give the evaluator's bits with GCC and clang. Math is V8's fdlibm translated to C by compiler/math/to-c.ts: Node's bits on 140,000 inputs. C runs a call's arguments in any order (GCC right to left), so later stages put the impure ones in locals first | #311 |
| C11 | G17's copy per type for generics used at several types, measured and left: what a type changes in compiled JS is equality, and none of the 17 example programs with a main compiles a generic `$eq` any more (converter's one compares Optionals, not a generic); the one-type case (round 2) was already within noise on chapters, since `$eq` answers texts and numbers at once. Reopen with a program where a generic compare at two types is hot | #310 |
| C21 | G1's full fix: recursion that isn't a tail call goes on the heap in compiled JS, so pages and imported modules go past about 1,500 levels. A definition in a cycle of calls gets a generator copy whose calls in the cycle yield; past 1,000 such calls on the stack the call runs that copy under `$drive`, which keeps the waiting calls in an array (Kotlin's DeepRecursiveFunction, trampolined style; credited). calc takes 100,000 nested parentheses on Node's default stack (845 ms; before, it stopped near 1,500), and its sessions take the same time. Shallow calls pay a counter; a catch that goes on, and each page update, puts it back | #309 |
| C7 | WebAssembly for full programs (WasmGC), all five stages of docs/design/targets/WASM.md: data, closures, text and the world (also as WASI commands with no JavaScript, Math included), searches and effects, Int past 64 bits. The last W1 item, one cast per arm rather than per field read, measured and left: V8 with every cast skipped (`--experimental-wasm-assume-ref-cast-succeeds`) runs Countdown in WebAssembly no faster (79-88 ms against 74-88, noise), so fewer casts can't buy anything | #308 |
| C7 W3e | Math with no JavaScript host: under WASI, sin, cos, tan, exp, log, pow and atan2 are V8's port of fdlibm (what Node's Math runs; Sun's and V8's notices kept, credited) compiled by clang to WebAssembly and turned into runtime functions by compiler/math/gen.ts (generated src/wasm-math.ts, 10 functions), their tables and stack in a memory of their own (multi-memory, on by default in Node 22 and wasmtime). 350,000 inputs (any bits, edges, past 2²⁰·π/2) give exactly Node's bits. A JavaScript host still imports Math, so a page matches the browser's evaluator. WebAssembly modules now need nothing from a host but the world | #307 |
| C7 W1d | Folds over nested data in WebAssembly: a field holding the type inside a container (a rose tree's List of children, a List of Lists) folds to that container with each one folded, one function per container type made by the fold (Coq's nested inductive types, as the evaluator's nested.ts; credited), tail recursive modulo cons so 50,000 children need no stack; with Choice too. A type inside itself at other arguments (List (List Tree)) is now in the profile | #306 |
| C7 W5c | Quotient, remainder and power on big Ints in WebAssembly: Knuth's algorithm D on 32-bit limbs (as Hacker's Delight writes it; credited), floor division with the divisor's sign as int.ts does; power by squaring on the big multiply. The smallest i64 over -1 is 2⁶³ now, not a trap. Matches BigInt on 57,000 random divisions (limbs chosen to reach the rare add-back step) and the evaluator in the test. An Int no longer traps anywhere but a power too big for memory | #305 |
| C7 W5b | Int in WebAssembly grows past 64 bits: past an i64 it is a `$huge`, a sign and 32-bit limbs, written in WebAssembly (add, subtract, multiply, compare, equal, show, read, as Number, whole part; Knuth's classical algorithms, credited), with i31 and i64 fast paths so the small case costs nothing measurable (wc the same time, 1.1 KB more). The host passes big ones limb by limb (`toInt`, `fromInt`). Matches the evaluator around the i31 and i64 edges and on random Ints up to 320 bits. Left: quotient, remainder and power past 2⁶³ still trap (W5c) | #304 |
| C24 | WebAssembly tail recursion modulo cons: a function whose answer ends as cons(x, itself(…)) keeps the items so far in one more input and turns them round onto the answer at its other ends, so each step is a tail call (reversing, not filling holes, as WasmGC cells are immutable). ledger's entries: 15,000 lines ran out of stack, 200,000 now take 1.1 s | #303 |
| C23 | WebAssembly folds over `characters(t)` with no list and no stack: one function walks t's bytes from the end, a character per tail call (fusion, Wadler credited); the host fills a text four bytes a call. wc on 100,000 lines 4.2 s → 0.38 s (11×) | #302 |
| C7 W5a | Int in WebAssembly is an `i31ref` while it fits in 31 bits, else a `$big` box (now an i64, limbs in W5b), as Hoot and wasm_of_ocaml keep small ints (credited); the runtime's Int functions stay on i64s behind generated wrappers, the host makes and reads Ints with `Int.of` and `Int.get`. No measurable cost on ledger and wc | #301 |
| C7 W3d | A Number's show needs no host: past the plain decimals it is JavaScript's String, with Ryū's shortest digits written in WebAssembly (adapted, credited; tables built with BigInt into one global) and ECMA-262's layout. The same as String on every binary exponent, the decimal powers and random bits (2.6 million checked once, 30,000 in the test); the `host.show` import is gone, so modules import only Math and the world | #299 |
| C7 W3b-2 | Programs as WASI commands, with no JavaScript: the world written in WebAssembly over `wasi_snapshot_preview1`, and `_start` running main (`npm run wasi -- wc` in compiler/). wc, ledger, chapters, calc and a program using the rest of the world give the evaluator's output, files and exit codes under `node:wasi` on 90 runs, and run under wasmtime | #289 |
| C7 W4d | Searches with several answers in WebAssembly: a function that chooses takes the rest of the search as a closure and calls it on each answer (Mercury's success continuations, as the JS `$k` copies); handlers keep answers in a mutable box. queens and Countdown run whole; Countdown at 1.0-1.1× the hand-written ideal JS (compiled JS 1.2-1.5×) | #293 |
| C7 W4b | Choosing in WebAssembly: in first, optional, all and count, lets that choose `one of` a list (keep's shape, and nested) are a loop per choice, a tail call per item in the evaluator's order (all collects backwards and turns the list round); functions with Choice as values give a status too, and a generic definition is lowered per effect row as well as per type (keep with a test that may have no answer is a search itself). Every pure function of chapters and calc at known types compiles; 207 of their calls and the new searches fixture match the evaluator, a keep over 2×10⁴ items in constant stack | #280 |
| C7 W4a | Searches with one answer or none in WebAssembly: a partial function (Choice, or spending from a budget) gives a status after its answer (Mercury's semidet calling convention, credited); a call checks it and branches to the nearest handler's block, a tail call to a function that gives the same stays `return_call`; `first`, `optional`, `all` and `count` are blocks; `fail`, `value of`, and the Choice builtins (quotient, remainder, power, whole part, read, find, character code, character) are runtime functions with a status. Running out of a budget is no answer where it happens, which the nearest handler sees, as in the evaluator (the shader profile keeps its count check). chapters' `heading` and `lower` and ledger's `newline` and `table` now compile; 515 inputs match the evaluator | #279 |
| C7 W3a | Int and Text in WebAssembly: Int an i64 whose overflow traps (a runtime error, big Ints are W5), Text a UTF-8 byte array with positions counted in characters; compiler/src/wasm-rt.ts is a small runtime written in WebAssembly's text format (concat, length, slice, split, join all, characters, comes before, equal, show of Int and Number, range) that an assembler there turns into bytes, so the module's text is what runs. Library definitions (map, keep) now lower at known types (their body's type variables differ from their type's). wc's, chapters' and ledger's text functions match the evaluator | #276 |
| C7 W2 | Functions as values in WebAssembly: a function type is a closure struct holding its code (`(ref $code)`) and a code type taking the closure and the inputs, one rec group; a lambda is a function of its own plus a closure struct of what it captured; a definition by name is a closure made once; a call of a function value is `call_ref`, `return_call_ref` in tail position; a constant is a function of no inputs. Queens' `safe` (a fold into a function), numbers' `factorial` (a constant), calculus `newton` (takes functions) compile; answers match the evaluator | #273 |
| C7 W1c | Structural `equal` on data in WebAssembly (the evaluator's rule on each number, the last field a tail call: two lists of a million compare in constant stack); a fold whose arms each fold one child (lists, chains) is a loop, `down` keeping a stack of the values passed and `up` giving each its arm, the evaluator's order: polynomial over 10⁴ coefficients overflowed the stack, now 10⁶ run (375 ms) | #271 |
| C7 W1b | Data in WebAssembly (`data`): types with several cases and types that contain themselves (lists, expression trees) as a base struct with the case's number and a subtype per case, one recursion group; a match is `br_table`; a fold is a recursive function of its own, a result used once computed where it's used (insert stops at its place); a module that may not finish runs as in JS. sort, insert, polynomial, ∂polynomial/∂x, Countdown's valid and combine, algebra's derivative give the evaluator's answers through exported makers and readers (`List.cons`, `List.case`, `List.cons.head`) | #269 |
| C7 W1a | Records in WebAssembly as WasmGC structs, an exported reader per field (`Colour.red`), a generic definition at two types exported under both; the ray marcher's colours match the evaluator exactly. The editor's CPU fallback could now use WebAssembly for colour records (Inbox) | #265 |
| C7 design | docs/design/targets/WASM.md: whole programs to WasmGC by growing the scalar profile towards the heap; representation table, text as UTF-8 arrays, Int past 64 bits from the host, the five stages | #262 |
| C6 | G13 again, the part left: a single-answer function whose answers are one record leaves their fields in `$got` (a `$fields` copy) when its caller goes on with the answer; Countdown's `join` builds no `found`, 1.47× → 1.30× the ideal | #259 |
| C22 | G23's last part measured and left: `entries` kept as an array for the folds from the end made 50,000 lines 146 ms against 138 ms (noise), since each step's `add to` is the cost, not the spine (GAPS.md G23) | #255 |
| C22 | G23 part 2: a list from `split` matched and handed back to its own definition (ledger's `entries`, calc's `run lines`) is the array and an index; the folds from the end are still open above | #246 |
| C16 | G10: the output's indentation follows its brackets, not the ones in text or comments | #243 |
| C15 | G2: equality with a value built in place is a tag test and its fields, with nothing built | #240 |
| C14 | G12: `one of` a few constants written in place runs each in turn, with no list | #238 |
| C12 | G18: a handler whose cases all escape is an inline try/catch (calc enters one per line; gain within noise) | #233 |
| C13 | G1 again: a program `lab compile` writes spends fuel only in calls that can be under a `with fuel` (calc has no checks left; the gain is within noise) | #230 |
| C2 | G21 part 3: a list from `split` taken apart by matches is an index into the array (ledger's `parse` makes no cells, about 5 to 10%); the rest is C22 | #228 |
| C8 | `Float32`: an approximate representation of `Number` (every job rounded to binary32, literals too), so f32 is in the program rather than the GPU's choice; on the GPU test, 578 of 1001 values (functions of + − × only) now match the evaluator exactly instead of closely. Design #220, builtin #224, representation #225 | #226 |
| C10 | Deep recursion checked: `lab compile` takes 1,000,000 nested parentheses; on the default stack compiled and the ideal both stop near 1,500, no gap; heap continuations left as C21 | #218 |
| C9 | Compiled JS expands `derivative` and `gradient` of a known function at compile time, as the scalar profile does; only a function known at run time asks the runtime's `$derivative` | #217 |
| C6 | G13 again, the fold part: a fold whose answer is one record carries its fields in locals and builds it once (chapters' `sections`, one record fewer per line) | #214 |
| L2 | Fuel in plain code inside a search: running out fails that branch only, as in the evaluator, instead of ending the whole `with fuel` body (guards only in programs that use `with fuel`) | #210 |
| C4 | G3 again: Bool parameters of definitions only called by name, and tests written in place for a fused `keep`, are JS booleans (ledger's `goes before`, chapters' `slug` and `words`) | #208 |
| C5 | G24 and G14 again: Choice builtins are partial (`$partial`, one answer or none), and `first` over a body with one answer runs it in place (ledger's `amount`, chapters' `lower`) | #204 |
| C20 | G25 list cells nobody else holds changed in place (`$own` copies): ledger's `by account` 35 → 25 ms, about 10% overall | #202 |
| C19 | G22 tail recursion modulo cons: `cons(x, self(rest))` fills holes in a loop, so ledger runs 50,000 lines on the default stack | #200 |
| C3 | GAPS round 3: ledger, 32 definitions built in the editor by clicks; compiled 2 to 4× the ideal; G22 (stack), G23, G24 and eight editor frictions | #197 |
| C2 | G21 parts 1 and 2: lists walked whole, and accumulators, stay arrays (chapters about 3.4× → 1.9× the ideal) | #182, #188 |
| C1 | G16 fusion: steps over a list run as one loop (chapters 550 → 200 ms) | #176 |
