Counting every call was weighed and not taken: a refactoring would change fuel answers just the same, and every compiled call would pay a count | #1327 |

Nothing looks at types at run time: where each is used both types are known, and a code → code pass (src/lang/fields-by-type.ts, run with arith.ts's) gives the call a generated definition with one arm per case, so the evaluator, relate.ts and every target see an ordinary match. The checker asks for both types where it is used (once its inputs are written: with holes, the holes say so), so a definition generic in the value's type can't use them (a later step could copy such definitions per type, as arith.ts does for + − ·). One level deep: a `List Arg` field is kept. Prior art: GHC's `deriving Functor`, "Scrap your boilerplate" (Lämmel and Peyton Jones, TLDI 2003) gmapT. Plain data only, like show, and the names are the program's, as show's are (the compiled program carries them only when it uses one); run backwards, it runs forwards once its value is known, as show does. machine's `op name` is one call instead of 21 arms. Each lane shows its logical board beside what it displays. Menu and apps page next (I38) | this PR |

| L83b | A backwards search stops when a recursion can't reach its answer any more: before a recursive call runs with its answer and some inputs known, its body is read as arithmetic on intervals with widening (src/lang/bounds.ts, Cousot and Cousot 1977), and a branch whose answer is outside what the call can return is dropped. `fibFast(n) = 8` ends after 17 steps with n = 5 only, and 9 says no answer after 7 (both ran out of budget before); a count down stops past its answer. Silent on anything it doesn't read (a match on data, another function, text), so `fib`'s tree search is as before. test/backwards.test.ts, BACKWARDS.md | #492 |

| L88d | Polynomial equations run backwards at Algebraic (EXACT-REALS.md step 3): an Algebraic + − · −x with an unknown input is also an exact equation (relate.ts `exactRoots`), the unknowns but one replaced by what they are made of, and the polynomial's real roots (algebraic.ts `realRoots`) are the answers, smallest first: x · x = 2 gives −√2 and √2, x · x + x = 12 gives −4 and 3, x⁴ − 10x² = −1 gives ±√2 ± √3, x · x = −1 none. Evaluated and compiled. test/clifford.test.ts | #550 |

Evaluated and compiled. test/clifford.test.ts | #552 |
| L89a | Fixed, step 1: `std.fixed`, a record around an Int of 2⁻³² steps, joins the one + − · < through `arithmetic at fixed` and `ordered at fixed` in the prelude. · is the steps' product shifted down 32 by a new Int builtin, `shifted down` (rounded towards −∞). A literal at Fixed is its digits rounded once to the nearest step. `as fixed`, `fixed as float64`, `show fixed`. A 1,000-step falling ball gives the same steps evaluated, compiled and by hand, and 10¹² plus one step minus 10¹² is one step. test/fixed.test.ts | #553 |
| L89b | Fixed shows exactly: `show fixed` is the shortest decimal that reads back to the same steps (Steele and White's free-format digits, 1990, in Ints: a fold over ten digits), so 0.1 shows as 0.1 and every step differently. `divide fixed` is floor(a · 2³² / b), no answer for 0. The test reads each shown decimal back and checks the two one digit shorter don't. test/fixed.test.ts | #560 |
| L89c | Trig at Fixed: `trig at fixed` gives the one sin, cos, tan, exp and log at Fixed, in Ints only. sin and cos by CORDIC (Volder, 1959; 32 turns after reducing the angle to [−π/2, π/2]), exp as e^r · 2ᵏ by its series, log as e·ln 2 + 2·atanh((m − 1)/(m + 1)), and `square root fixed` by Heron's method. Within a few steps of the true value and the same evaluated and compiled. Two edges give a number because Trig can't refuse: log of 0 or less is 0, tan at cos = 0 is sin · 2³² steps. test/fixed.test.ts | #565 |

| L96a | Ways, step 1: a def's optional `way` names the interface field it computes, `by its ways` fills an implementation's field, and arith.ts picks per implementation the cheapest way that ends in given fields (Knuth's 1977 generalisation of Dijkstra, each given field 1, a way 1 plus its uses), so a cycle is followed only from a given field. The prelude's first ways are `cos by sin` and `sin by cos`; a Trig that gives either gets the other, and one that gives neither is refused, naming the field. Evaluated and compiled. WAYS.md step 1. test/ways.test.ts | #577 |

| L67 | `Outcome a = done(value) \| refused(reason)` in std, the data form of Abort; prelude `attempt(action)` and `value or abort(o)`, plain matches; LANGUAGE.md "`Outcome`: the data form of Abort" | #385 |

| L76 | Maps as a weight-balanced tree (Adams 1993), `withMapTree` in src/lang/map-tree.ts: a representation of L65's Map, `canonical: false`, put/value at/remove key/has key in log n, checked against the meaning with the balance after each step; chooser picks it for a word count (3.5× on 1,200 words). Automatic choice and equal/show through meaning are C47 | #391 |
| L84 | Recursive lets: a let's function may call itself by the let's name and use the inputs around it (ML's `let rec`); checked for termination like a definition; src/lang/lift-lets.ts lifts it into a definition before the evaluator, relate.ts and every target run (via resolveArithmetic); the palette offers the name inside; the scope error now says "has no value here"; evaluator, JS, WebAssembly and C tests | #460 |

| L81 | Infinities equal only to themselves: `close` (and compiled JS's `$near`, WebAssembly, C and WGSL's near) counts only a finite gap as near, so `equal(∞, −∞)`, `equal(∞, 0)` and `equal(1e308, ∞)` are false (newton on 1/x in examples/algebra used to stop on ∞ = 0 and now runs on, within `root near`'s budget); evaluator, JS, WebAssembly and C tests | #453 |
| L80 | Ints that know they're Ints: an Int is a JS number only below 2^28 and a BigInt beyond (int.ts), so `sameNumber` is near on every JS number with no wholeness test and exact on BigInts; √1e17·√1e17 = 1e17 now holds; compiled JS literals, WebAssembly and C's near, and the boundary tests follow | #446 |

| L56 | Effects take type parameters (`Yield a`, `State s`), as in Koka, Unison and Effekt: a row holds applied effects (types.ts `Row.args`, by effect id, so code reading `effs` is unchanged), unifying two rows unifies the arguments of an effect in both, a handler's cases see the computation's types, and a definition is generic over them (`pass on(x) = yield(x)` is `(a) -[Yield a]-> Unit`). The compilers still key handlers by id. How a type says its `·` is still Ersagun's call (L40b). test/complex.test.ts | #488 |

| L86d | A definition is at its own defaults, so a use at another type gets a copy: since L86b made `inc(x) = x + 1` an Int function, a use at Float64 still called it (arith.ts `instance` skipped the copy for Float64), so `inc(2.5)` ran Int's + and `0.5 · inc(0)` was refused on the GPU ("takes or gives Int"). Found building the Mandelbrot shader (L40b). test/arith.test.ts | #481 |
| L90 | Int polynomials run backwards: with two or more sides unknown, an Int add, subtract, negate or multiply is also an equation for the solver, its unknowns whole (an answer near a whole number is it, one that isn't is none), and the operation's own wait still checks each answer exactly; so `x · x + x = 12` gives 3 and −4 again, as at Float64. The solver reads a big Int as the nearest float. test/backwards.test.ts, BACKWARDS.md | #479 |
| L86c | Int multiply runs backwards (a regression from L86b, where a whole literal became an Int): a factor from the other and out when it divides exactly, `x · x` from its whole square roots, so `x · 3 + 1 = 13` gives 4 again; int.ts `divides`, `squareRoots`; test/backwards.test.ts; BACKWARDS.md. Other Int polynomials are L90 | #477 |
| L39b | Fractions run backwards exactly: relate.ts runs the prelude's `add`/`subtract`/`multiply`/`divide fractions` and `negate fraction` as one relation each, any two sides giving the third (`x·(1/3) + 1/2 = 5/6` gives `x = 1`; `0·x = 0` leaves `x` free). `lowest terms` in WASM and C was C33; the GPU has no Ints, so fractions stay off it. test/rational.test.ts, BACKWARDS.md, NUMBERS.md | #476 |
| L75 | Literals at Rational (`of: "std.rational"`, a whole number or `"top/bottom"`, put in lowest terms; a zero bottom is a checker error) and at Complex (`of: "std.complex"`, the real part), drawn as written and spelled as their constructor's call by arith.ts before anything runs; the language half of QA-2026-10-24b's "ratio(1, 2) + 1 is refused", E69 is the palette half | #381 |

Deeper needs more than the GPU's f32 | #524 |

Now a smoothstep triangle wave, only + − · abs mod and one power, 0.0005 from the evaluator | #527 |

Corrects L93's reasoning: the error is cos's, in any form, not 1 − cos's cancellation | #528 |

| D9 | Compass: a perpendicular from a circle's own centre, or an angle bisector with a side point on the corner, drew a line pointing wherever rounding said, swinging as points moved (Ersagun 2026-11-02). Its vector is 0; such a line is now nothing, and the help says so | #1098 |
| D8 | Compass: an Angle bisector tool, three clicks (a point on one side, the corner, a point on the other). Each side as a line of size 1 running from the corner, l / √(l · l); their sum halves the angle. Test: two bisectors of a triangle cross at a point equally far from all three sides | #1092 |
| D7 | Compass: a Perpendicular tool. Click a point, then a line: (p ∧ s ∧ ∞)*, the line through p meeting s at right angles (dot 0 with p, s and ∞); with a circle it is the line through the point and the centre | #1090 |
| D6 | Compass: a Midpoint tool (Ersagun 2026-11-02, after building an incircle by hand). Two points p and q of weight 1: p − q is their perpendicular bisector (x · p = x · q), and the midpoint is where it crosses the line through them. Perpendicular and angle bisector follow as D7, D8 | #1088 |
| I147 | Compass: a circle round a point through a point where two lines cross missed that point, and shrank as the crossing moved away from the origin (Ersagun 2026-11-02, building an incircle). Two lines' crossing was read off the flat point as o ⌋ T = x − ½\|x\|² ∞, which has the point's x and y but isn't a point (it doesn't square to 0), so c · p was off by ½\|x\|². The crossing is made again from its x and y; test/compass.test.ts | #1079 |
| D5 | The guitar pedal restyled as a stompbox (Ersagun 2026-10-30): the converter's colours with a dark version; the two sources light up while they play (the page's `data-playing`); the controls are the pedal's dark top, its switch lit orange while driven (a class worked out from `driven`), the drive in a monospace readout; the waveform a scope screen with a grid. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class | this PR |
| D4 | Pig restyled in the converter's look (Ersagun 2026-10-30): its colours with a dark version, Play a game the filled button, the note on the handlers a soft card, the game's lines a card with the last line (who won) bold; the empty log and tally take no room before a game. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class, no inline styles left | this PR |
| D3 | Two dice restyled in the converter's look (Ersagun 2026-10-30: the HTML demos look too primitive): the converter's colours with a dark version, a filled Roll button, the chart a card with rounded green bars on a track and the red tick for fair dice. A `styles` definition of rules and every element named by its class; the only inline styles left are a bar's width and a tick's place, which are computed. The unit converter (D2) already had its own style sheet and this look, so it is unchanged | this PR |
| D1 | The nested to-do restyled (Ersagun 2026-10-30: long named buttons on each item, the tick a separate button, add a step taking the top field's title). The tick is the round box before the title; ↑, + and × are small buttons that show on hover; + opens a field under the item for the step's title (Enter adds, Escape closes); a count and a bar at the top. | C63e | A bug in the one-holder check (one-holder.ts): the same nodes walked twice, as routes' `near` does with its fold (a match's scrutinee, and its otherwise again), counted the second walk's fold result as used after it was changed, so routes' sets failed. The names an arm binds are new each time it runs, so what an earlier walk used up under them no longer counts. routes' sets keep the tree all the same: their items are pairs, and a table keeps Int and Text keys. Tests: ters/test/one-holder.test.ts | #910 |

The chooser takes it where a program changes items by position in a loop; `List as written` now costs `replace at` too, and the list boundary for builtins goes back through the row's own nil and cons when it isn't the array (library-reps.ts). Binary, where RRB (the row's name) is 32-wide: same costs. Test: library-reps.test.ts (a set in pairs mapped over keeps the tree, prints the same; one shown through a helper still keeps the definition) | #926 |
| C40 (main's waits) | Compiled JavaScript answers what main leaves unhandled of Await, as the evaluator's runtime does (L71c): the runtime's `$main` drives main's generator, `start timer(ms)` gives a ticket ready at the world's clock plus ms, and awaiting a ticket, a timer or `any of(…)` waits until the least ready time and goes on with its value. The world's `wait` sleeps; a world with none gets a promise of main's value, resumed by setTimeout. `lab compile`'s program uses it, so a compiled main that waits runs at all (before, its generator was never started). test/await.test.ts: together, race and within compiled give the evaluator's prints and waits | #958 |
| C73 | Checked, put back: `take(sort by(xs, key), n)` as a selection (keep the best n in a sorted list of at most n, each item put in front of the first kept one whose key isn't before its own, so ties come out as the stable sort leaves them). It gave the sort's answers in a test, but notebook never writes that shape where it counts (`hits text` takes 5 of a list sorted in `ranked`, and uses its own Int `take`), so reading it there needs inlining across definitions for a few percent; not kept (GAPS.md round 6 N5) | #957 |
| C72 (hash-table side) | std.ts's `Table` keeps each version's sorted entries (a set's items) the first time `entries of` asks: a version never changes, so a map read many times, as notebook's postings are, is sorted once. Notebook's table run at 3000 notes: 700 ms to 510 (median of 21). test/table.test.ts checks every listed version against a copy per version. The trees' side (walking in order) stays on C72's row | #947 |
| C71 | The hash table without one holder per map (GAPS.md round 6 N2), another way than the row's per-value choice: every version of std.ts's `Table` stays a value. A put or remove writes into the one JS Map and turns the version it was given into the change that undoes it; reading an old version undoes the changes between (Baker's rerooting; Conchon and Filliâtre). So library-reps.ts no longer asks one-holder.ts, only that keys are Int or Text. notebook at 3000 notes compiled: Map now a hash table too, 8× → 6× the ideal; routes unchanged within noise. Tests: ters/test/table.test.ts (4000 random changes and reads over branching versions agree with a copy per version), compiler/test/hash-table.test.ts (a map and a set whose old version is read again get the table and print the same) | #931 |
